weight, it is only a dyadic
\mathbf{A}_{2}
weight, and the periodization does not make the
\mathbf{A}_{2}

characteristic better. Fortunately, a slight modification of the iterated periodization, which we
will call the
quasi-periodization
allows us to get the
\mathbf{A}_{2}
condition on all intervals.

Namely, for a function
f
on
I^{0}
with finite Haar expansion we get its quasi-periodization as
follows.

Recall that in the periodization a starting interval
I
of order
k
is divided into
2^{N_{k}}

dyadic subintervals, which we called
stopping intervals
of order
k
. Let us call the
stopping intervals touching the boundary of
I
the
exceptional
stopping intervals (there
are
2
of them), and the rest are the
regular
stopping intervals. We use the notation

\mathcal{E}(I)
and
\mathcal{R}(I)
for exceptional and regular stopping subintervals respectively.

To get the function
f_{k}
from
f_{k-1}
on the starting interval
I
, we, as in the case of
iterated periodization, compute
\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}f_{k-1}
,
but on the exceptional stopping subintervals
I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}(I)
we put the values of the function
f_{k}

to
be
\langle f_{k-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\langle\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}f_{k-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}
; on the regular stopping
intervals

I^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}(I)
we still have
f_{k}=\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}f_{k-1}
. The result on the interval
I
will
be denoted by
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}f_{k-1}
; note that it differs from
\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}f_{k-1}

only on two exceptional
stopping subintervals of
I
.

We then will do nothing on the exceptional stopping intervals, and continue on the regular
ones: take their grandchildren as the starting intervals, and so on….

The process will stop after finitely many steps, and we denote the result as
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}f
.

Note that the quasi-periodization is not given by a composition of the original function with a
measure
preserving transformation.
However, exactly as in the case of iterated periodization, we will assign to each starting and
each stopping interval
I
of order
k
a unique interval
\mathcal{F}(I)\in\mathscr{D}_{2k-1}
, see end of
Section
7.1
. Formally, the function
\mathcal{F}
is the restriction of the function
described above in Section
7.1
, because it is not defined on any of the descendants of
the exceptional stopping intervals. However, to simplify typography again (and because we will not
be using this function for periodization anymore) we will be using the same
symbol
\mathcal{F}
for it.

We can compare the iterated periodization and the iterated quasi-periodization quantitatively.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
f
be a function with finite Haar expansion, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}f-\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\longrightarrow 0\qquad\text{as }\vec{N}\to\infty.

Proof.

Since
f
with finite Haar expansion are bounded, the difference above is controlled by the
total size of the exceptional intervals, which is arbitrarily small as
\vec{N}\to\infty
.
∎

7.2.2.
An alternative description of the iterated quasi-periodization

Let us analyze the action of the quasi-periodization on martingale differences and write an
analogue of
formula (
7.4
) for the (iterated) quasi-periodization. Applying the iterated
quasi-periodization to the martingale difference decomposition (
7.3
), we get
almost the same formula as (
7.4
) with a slight modification. Namely, denote by

\operatorname{Start}^{\prime}\subset\operatorname{Start}
the collection of all starting intervals that are not a subset of any
exceptional stopping interval. Then it is not hard to see that for
J\in\mathscr{D}_{2k-1}

(7.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}f=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\in\operatorname{Start}^{\prime}:\\
\mathcal{F}(I)=J\end{subarray}}\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}\left(\left(\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}f\right)\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J,I}\right).

Looking at this formula and the martingale difference decomposition (
7.3
), we
can see that for a function
f
with finite Haar expansion its quasi-periodization
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}f
can be obtained by starting with the function
\mathbf{E}_{1}f
(which is constant on
I^{0}_{\pm}
) and then
applying recursively on starting intervals the following procedure finitely many times:

Procedure 7.3
.

On an interval
I
where the previously constructed function is constant, add to this
function a quasi-periodization
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{N(I)}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{2}\varphi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}
of an appropriate second
order martingale difference.

The order of starting intervals where we apply the above procedure is not really important: it
just needs to agree with the ordering by inclusion of the intervals. Namely, we can apply the
procedure to a starting interval only after we are done with all starting intervals containing it.

7.2.3.
Remodeling

In our case we apply the
quasi-periodization to the weights
W
,
V=W^{-1}
and the functions
\mathbf{f}
,
\mathbf{g}
; recall that we
assumed that all the objects have finite Haar expansions. We get the quasi-periodization of all
objects,
but while we do not need to worry about
\widetilde{\mathbf{f}}:=\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}:=\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}
, we have a small problem with the quasi-periodizations of the weights: on the exceptional
stopping
intervals the weights
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}V
and
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}W
are not inverses of each other
(but they are everywhere else).

Fortunately, this problem is very easy to fix. After performing the (iterated) quasi-periodization,
we got a
disjoint collection of exceptional stopping intervals, where we have a problem. So, on each such
exceptional stopping interval

I
of order
k
we replace constants by appropriately chosen weights
and perform the iterated quasi-periodization of these
weights.
We will get a new collection of exceptional stopping intervals, perform iterated
quasi-periodization of appropriate weights on these intervals, and so on…

To write it down formally, recall that during the quasi-periodization,
as it was discussed above at the end of Section
7.2.1
, we associate with each stopping
interval
I
of order
k
a unique interval
J=\mathcal{F}(I)\in\mathscr{D}_{2k-1}
. And on the exceptional
stopping intervals we perform
the iterated quasi-periodization
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{2}}
of the weights
V\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J,I}
,

W\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J,I}
,
J=\mathcal{F}(I)
; here
\vec{2}:=(2,2,2,\ldots)
.

We get a new set of exceptional stopping intervals, and their total length will be
1/2
of the
total length of the previous stopping intervals. On these new stopping intervals
3
3

3

the map
\mathcal{F}
is naturally extended to new stopping and starting intervals.

we again perform
the iterated quasi-periodization
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{2}}
of the corresponding weights, again treating
these stopping intervals, and not their grandchildren, as starting ones (of the same order), get
new stopping intervals, and so on.

In every step of the remodeling iterations, we will get new
exceptional stopping intervals, but their total measure will be half of the measure of the
previous stopping intervals.

Therefore, inductively repeating this procedure
and taking the limit,
4
4

4

By the construction the limit exists a.e., and since
W
and
V=W^{-1}
have finite Haar
expansion, the limit exists in (matrix-valued)
L^{1}
.

we will end up with the weights
\widetilde{V}
,
\widetilde{W}
such that
\widetilde{V}=\widetilde{W}^{-1}
a.e.

We will denote the new remodeled weights as
\widetilde{V}:=\varrho^{\vec{N}}V
,
\widetilde{W}:=\varrho^{\vec{N}}W
.

Remark 7.4
.

We would like to emphasize that in the remodeling we only change the weights; the functions

\widetilde{\mathbf{f}}
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}
will be the quasi-periodizations
\widetilde{\mathbf{f}}=\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}
,

\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}=\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}
, and will not be changed any further. We will use Lemma

7.9
below to estimate
\left(\mathcal{H}\widetilde{\mathbf{f}},\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}
, so we will need to
pick appropriately large frequencies
N_{k}
later.
As for the behavior of the weight, all the modifications after the first quasi-periodization do not
influence estimates of
\left(\mathcal{H}\widetilde{\mathbf{f}},\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}
, and we will not need arbitrarily
large frequencies. We are performing the remodeling to make the weights
\widetilde{V}
and
\widetilde{W}
to be
the inverses of each other, and for this purpose any frequency
N\geq 2
(the same for all
exceptional intervals) will work. So above we just picked
N=2
.

Remark 7.5
.

As we discussed above, the remodeling in our construction was done by performing consecutively
quasi-periodizations on some intervals on some functions with finite Haar expansion, and
quasi-periodization is done applying the above Procedure
7.3
finitely many times.

Further analyzing the remodeling, we can see that the remodeling of the function
f
can be
obtained by
starting with the function
\mathbf{E}_{1}f:=\langle f\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}+\langle f\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}
(which is constant on intervals
I_{\pm}^{0}
), then
applying inductively Procedure
7.3
and taking the limit (a.e. and/or in
L^{1}
).

Again, the order of starting intervals, where we apply the above procedure is not really
important and it
only needs to agree with the ordering by inclusion of the intervals. Namely, we can apply the
procedure to a starting interval only after we are done with all starting intervals containing it.

The picture below illustrates the iterated quasi-periodisation process and should be compared with the iterated periodisation process from Fig.
4
.

Figure 5.

The first intervals on which quasi-periodisation is applied are again

\operatorname{Start}_{1}=\{I^{0}_{-},I^{0}_{+}\}
.
The function
f|_{I^{0}_{-}}
is copied
2^{N_{1}}-2=6
times on the
6
regular stopping intervals
\mathcal{R}(I^{0}_{-})
in
\mathcal{D}_{4}\cap{I^{0}_{-}}
that are
not
touching the boundary of
I^{0}_{-}
. The two blue intervals from
\mathcal{D}_{4}\cap{I^{0}_{-}}
that are touching the boundary of
I^{0}_{-}

are the two exceptional stopping intervals
\mathcal{E}(I^{0}_{-})
below
I^{0}_{-}
,
the red intervals are
\mathcal{E}(I^{0}_{+})
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
\mathbf{f}
,
\mathbf{g}
,
W
,
W^{-1}
be functions with finite Haar expansions, and let
W{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!\!\vec{N}}:=\varrho^{\vec{N}}W
be the remodeling of the weight
W
. Then

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\varrho^{\vec{N}}W)}\longrightarrow\|\mathbf{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)},\qquad\left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}((\varrho^{\vec{N}}W)^{-1})}\longrightarrow\|\mathbf{g}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}.

Proof.

Since the function
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}
is constant on the exceptional stopping intervals, the
contribution of these intervals to the norm
\left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\varrho^{\vec{N}}W)}
depends only on the averages of the weight
\varrho^{\vec{N}}W
there. Therefore

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\varrho^{\vec{N}}W)}=\left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}W)}=\left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}W)}.

We also trivially have
\left\|\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}W)}=\|\mathbf{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}
. Because of the finite Haar expansion the weight
W
is bounded, and applying
Lemma
7.2
we get the first limit.

Noticing that
(\varrho^{\vec{N}}W)^{-1}=\varrho^{\vec{N}}(W^{-1})
we immediately get the second limit.
∎

7.3.
Hilbert Transforms and periodization

Recall that the Hilbert Transform on the real line
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}f(s)=\frac{1}{\pi}\text{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{f(t)}{s-t}{\mathrm{d}}t

and the Hilbert Transform
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{T}}
on the torus
\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}/\penalty\mathbb{Z}
(the periodic Hilbert Transform)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{T}}f(s)=\text{p.v.}\int_{0}^{1}f(t)\cot\left(\pi\cdot(s-t)\right){\mathrm{d}}t;

here we naturally identify
\mathbb{T}
with
I^{0}=[0,1)
with the Lebesgue measure
{\mathrm{d}}x
. We can also
identify
\mathbb{T}
with an arbitrary interval
I
with the normalized Lebesgue measure
|I|^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}x
.
In this case the Hilbert Transform on the torus
\mathbb{T}
is represented as

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}f(s)=|I|^{-1}\text{p.v.}\int_{I}f(t)\cot\left(|I|^{-1}\pi\cdot(s-t)\right){\mathrm{d}}t,\qquad s\in I.

If we denote
L^{2}(I)=L^{2}(I,|I|^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}x)
, then the representations
\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}
in
L^{2}(I)

and
\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{\mathbb{T}}
in
L^{2}(J)
are unitarily equivalent,

(7.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}U{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I,J}=U{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I,J}\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{\mathbb{T}},

where the unitary operator
U{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I,J}:L^{2}(J)\to L^{2}(I)
is given by

(7.8)

\displaystyle U{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I,J}f=f\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J,I}.

Define

(7.9)

\displaystyle c_{1}:=\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})},\qquad c_{2}:=\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}.

Recall, that extending a function
f\in L^{2}(I^{0})
periodically to the
real line
\mathbb{R}
, we naturally identify
I^{0}
with the torus
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
and the space

L^{2}(I^{0})
with
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
.
The rotation of the torus
\mathbb{T}
through
\pi
, i.e. map
f(\,\cdot\,)\mapsto f(\,\cdot\,+1/2)

in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}))=L^{2}(I^{0})
is a unitary transformation in
L^{2}(I^{0})
, mapping
h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}
to

-h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}
, and
h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}
to
h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}
, so we can write

(7.10)

\displaystyle c_{1}=-\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}.

Let the dyadic operator
\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}
be defined as

(7.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}:=c_{1}(\mathbb{S}-\mathbb{S}^{*})+c_{2}\mathbb{S}_{0}.

Let also

(7.12)

\displaystyle c_{0}:=\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}},\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}.

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
\mathbf{f}
,
\mathbf{g}
be functions on
I^{0}=[0,1)
with finite Haar expansion. Then

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f},\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}\right)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\longrightarrow\left(\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}+c_{0}\left[\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}-\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\right]

along some family of
\vec{N}\to\infty

(
all
N_{k}\to\infty
)
.

Remark 7.8
.

It is possible to show that in fact we have the convergence as
\vec{N}\to\infty
, but for our
purposes convergence along some family is sufficient.

Using Lemma
7.2
we can trivially get from
the above Lemma
7.7

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
\mathbf{f}
,
\mathbf{g}
be functions on
I^{0}=[0,1)
with finite Haar expansion. Then

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f},\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}\right)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\longrightarrow\left(\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}+c_{0}\left[\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}-\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\right].

along some family of
\vec{N}\to\infty

(
all
N_{k}\to\infty
)
.

In both lemmas above the first term
\left(\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}
is the essential one
that will be used, and the second term is small in comparison.

To prove Lemma
7.7
we will need some additional results. The first one explains the
connection between the operator
\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}
and the periodic Hilbert Transform
\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}
.

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
I\in\mathscr{D}
.
For all
f,g\in\operatorname{Ran}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{2}
,

(7.13)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}f,g\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}=\left(\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}f,g\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}.

Proof.

First of all notice that the functions
|I|^{1/2}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}
,
|I|^{1/2}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}
,
|I|^{1/2}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}
form an orthonormal basis for
\operatorname{Ran}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{2}
in the norm of
L^{2}(I)
, and that

\displaystyle h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}=|I|^{1/2}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I,I^{0}},\qquad h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{\pm}}=|I|^{1/2}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{\pm}}\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I,I^{0}}.

Identities
(
7.7
) mean that
\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}
in
L^{2}(I)
and
\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}^{\mathbb{T}}
in
L^{2}(I^{0})
are
unitarily equivalent

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}^{\mathbb{T}}U{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0},I}=U{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0},I}\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}},

where, recall,

U{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0},I}f=f\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I,I^{0}}
; note that
U{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0},I}
is a unitary
operator from
L^{2}(I)
to
L^{2}(I^{0})
.
Therefore the definitions (
7.9
), (
7.10
) of

c_{1}
,
c_{2}
can be rewritten as

(7.14)

\displaystyle c_{1}

\displaystyle:=\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}=|I|\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}=-\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})},

(7.15)

\displaystyle c_{2}

\displaystyle:=\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}=|I|\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}.

Decompose
f,g\in\operatorname{Ran}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{2}
as

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}|I|^{1/2}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}+x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}|I|^{1/2}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}+x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}|I|^{1/2}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}},

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}|I|^{1/2}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}+y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}|I|^{1/2}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}+y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}|I|^{1/2}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}.

Then

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}f,g\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=|I|\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}\left(x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}+x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}+x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\right),y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}+y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}+y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=|I|\begin{pmatrix}y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\\
y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\\
y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\end{pmatrix}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}&\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}&\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}\\
\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}&\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}&\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}\\
\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}&\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}&\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}},h{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\\
x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\\
x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\phantom{|I|}\begin{pmatrix}y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\\
y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\\
y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\end{pmatrix}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&c_{1}&-c_{1}\\
-c_{1}&0&c_{2}\\
c_{1}&-c_{2}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\\
x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\\
x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\end{pmatrix}.

Applying similar computations to
\left(\mathbb{S}f,g\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}
,
\left(\mathbb{S}^{*}f,g\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}
and
\left(\mathbb{S}_{0}f,g\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}
we get

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{S}f,g\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\\
y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\\
y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\end{pmatrix}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
-1&0&0\\
1&0&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\\
x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\\
x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{S}^{*}f,g\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\\
y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\\
y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\end{pmatrix}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1&1\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\\
x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\\
x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{S}_{0}f,g\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\\
y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\\
y{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\end{pmatrix}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&1\\
0&-1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\\
x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{-}}\\
x{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I_{+}}\end{pmatrix},

which gives the desired conclusion.
∎

Lemma 7.11
.

Let
f
be a function on an interval
I
,
\langle f\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=0
with finite Haar expansion on
I
,
extended by
0
outside of
I
. Then

(7.16)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{P}^{N}_{I}f-\mathcal{P}^{N}_{I}\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}f\right\|_{L^{2}(I)}=0,\qquad\lim_{N\to\infty}\left\|\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus I}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{P}^{N}_{I}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=0.

Proof.

Since the Hilbert Transforms
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}
and
\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}
commute with rescaling and translations,
it is sufficient to prove the lemma for
I=I^{0}=[0,1)
.

Let
g^{N}
be the function
\mathcal{P}^{N}_{I^{0}}f
extended by
1
-periodicity to
\mathbb{R}
. Then for any

f\in L^{2}(I^{0})
we have on
I^{0}

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}g^{N}=\mathcal{P}^{N}_{I^{0}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{T}}f;

the identity is trivial for the exponentials
e^{2\pi ikx}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, and extends to

L^{2}(I^{0})
by continuity. We then need to show that

(7.17)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus I^{0}})\right\|\to 0\qquad\text{as }N\to\infty.

But this is an easy exercise in basic analysis. Namely, denote
I^{0}_{m}:=[-2^{-m},1+2^{-m}]
. Then
using the fact that
\langle g^{N}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=0
for any interval
I
of length
2^{-N}
, we can easily
see that for a fixed
m\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}\setminus I^{0}_{m}})\rightrightarrows 0\quad\text{on }I^{0},\qquad\text{as }N\to\infty.

But
\|1{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{m}\setminus I^{0}}f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}}
can be made arbitrarily small, and together with the
boundedness of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}
this implies (
7.17
). Thus, the first identity in (
7.16
) is proved.

The second identity in (
7.16
) is proved similarly to (
7.17
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
7.7
.

Let
\operatorname{Start}=\operatorname{Start}^{\vec{N}}
be the collection of all starting intervals appearing in the
iterated periodization, and let
\operatorname{Start}_{k}=\operatorname{Start}^{\vec{N}}_{k}
be the collection of all starting
intervals of order
k
. Since the periodization does not change constant functions, using the
alternative description of the periodization in Section
7.1.1
we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\mathbf{f}+\mathbf{E}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\mathbf{f}+\sum_{k\geq 1}\ \sum_{I\in\operatorname{Start}_{k}}\mathcal{P}^{N_{k}}_{I}\left(\left(\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathcal{F}(I)}^{2}\mathbf{f}\right)\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathcal{F}(I),I}\right),

\displaystyle=:\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{f}+\sum_{I\in\operatorname{Start}}D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f},

and similarly for
\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}
. Here, to simplify the write-up, we do not explicitly write
the
dependence of

(7.18)

\displaystyle D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f}:=\mathcal{P}^{N_{k}}_{I}\left(\left(\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathcal{F}(I)}^{2}\mathbf{f}\right)\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathcal{F}(I),I}\right)

on the generation
k
and the indices
N_{j}
,
j\leq k
, but we will
have it in mind.

Then we can write

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f},\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}\right)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}=\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}

\displaystyle+\sum_{I\in\operatorname{Start}}\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f},D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}

\displaystyle+\text{ cross terms.}

We will show later that we can pick the
N_{k}
sufficiently large, so the sum of the cross terms is
arbitrarily small. As for now, let us compute the limit of the above sum. We fix
I\in\operatorname{Start}_{k}
,
and let
J=\mathcal{F}(I)\in\mathscr{D}_{2k-1}
. Denote

\displaystyle f{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}:=\left(\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\mathbf{f}\right)\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J,I},\qquad g{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}:=\left(\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\mathbf{g}\right)\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J,I}.

Trivially,
f{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},g{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\in\operatorname{Ran}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{2}
and for a stopping interval
I
of order
k

we have

D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f}=\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}f{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\qquad D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{g}=\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}g{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}.

For one given
I
in the sum above, the term of interest writes also

\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f},D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}=\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f},D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}|I|,

where asymptotically

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f},D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}f{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}g{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle\to\left(\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}f{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{N_{k}}g{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}f{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},g{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}=\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{\mathbb{T}}\left(\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\mathbf{f}\right)\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J,I},\left(\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\mathbf{g}\right)\circ\psi{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J,I}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathcal{H}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{\mathbb{T}}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\mathbf{f},\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(J)}=\left(\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\mathbf{f},\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(J)},

and where the last equality follows from Lemma
7.10
.

To conclude with the diagonal terms, we collect contributions of all starting intervals
I\in\operatorname{Start}
in the main term and
estimate asymptotically when
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\operatorname{Start}}\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f},D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{I\in\operatorname{Start}_{k}}\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f},D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{I\in\operatorname{Start}_{k}}\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f},D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I)}|I|

\displaystyle\to\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{I\in\operatorname{Start}_{k}}\left(\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathcal{F}(I)}^{2}\mathbf{f},\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathcal{F}(I)}^{2}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\mathcal{F}(I))}|I|

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{J\in\mathscr{D}_{2k-1}}\sum_{I:\mathcal{F}(I)=J}\left(\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\mathbf{f},\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(J}|I|

\displaystyle=\sum_{J\in\mathscr{D}_{\scriptstyle\text{\rm odd}}}\left(\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\mathbf{f},\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(J)}|J|=\left(\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}\,;

here we used that the total measure of all starting intervals
I
such that
\mathcal{F}(I)=J
is
exactly
|J|
.

Finally, we can easily see that

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}=c_{0}\left[\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}-\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\right],

so if we can make the sum of the cross terms as small as we want, the lemma is proved.

To complete the proof, let us show that asymptotically, we can ignore the cross terms.
Since we have finitely many cross terms, it is sufficient to show that given small
\varepsilon>0

we can make each cross term bounded by
\varepsilon
.

First, using the second limit in Lemma
7.11
, we can see that for all starting
intervals

I
,
J
of order
1
,
I\neq J
, and for all sufficiently large
N_{1}
,

(7.19)

\displaystyle\left|\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f},D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}\right|<\varepsilon.

We know that functions
D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f}
,
D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{g}
are just periodizations of appropriate
functions, so for stopping intervals
I
of order
1
,

D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f}\to 0,\quad D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{g}\to 0\qquad\text{weakly as }N_{1}\to\infty.

That means that for all sufficiently large
N_{1}
,

\left|\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}\right|<\varepsilon,\qquad\left|\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{f},D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}\right|<\varepsilon

for all starting intervals
I
of order
1
.

Let us fix
N_{1}
such that all the inequalities hold.

We
then proceed by induction. We assume that we found indices
N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1}
such that all
the cross terms involving (only)
\mathbf{E}_{1}
and
D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}
with the starting intervals
I^{\prime}
of rank

\leq k-1
have the desired estimates. We now consider all the vectors

(7.20)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{f},\quad\mathbf{E}_{1}\mathbf{g},\quad D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}\mathbf{f},\quad D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime\prime}}\mathbf{g}

with
I
of rank
\leq k-1
, to be fixed at this step.

Again, using the second limit in Lemma
7.11
, we can see that for all starting
intervals

I
,
J
of order
k
,
I\neq J
, and for all sufficiently large
N_{k}
, the inequality (
7.19
) holds.

Since for all starting intervals
I
of rank
k

D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{f}\to 0,\quad D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\mathbf{g}\to 0\qquad\text{weakly as }N_{k}\to\infty,

we can find sufficiently large
N_{k}
such that all the inner products of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}\mathbf{f}
,

\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}D{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}\mathbf{g}
with the vectors fixed in (
7.20
) are bounded by
\varepsilon
.

Thus we can ignore the cross terms, and the lemma is proved.
∎

8.
Lower bound for the Hilbert Transform

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

The proof of the main theorem is now in order. We want to find functions
\widetilde{f}\in L^{2}(W)
,
\widetilde{g}\in L^{2}(W)
,
\|\widetilde{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}=\|\widetilde{g}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}=1
such that for

\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}\widetilde{f},\widetilde{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\gtrsim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}.

Denoting
\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}:=W\widetilde{g}
(and
\widetilde{\mathbf{f}}:=\widetilde{f}
for
consistency of notation), we can see that
the above estimate is
equivalent to

(8.1)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}\widetilde{\mathbf{f}},\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}\gtrsim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2},\qquad\|\widetilde{\mathbf{f}}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}=\|\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}=1

(with the same implied constant).

Recall that from Lemma
7.9
, we have
for any
\mathbf{f}
,
\mathbf{g}
with finite Haar expansion

(8.2)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f},\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}\right)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\longrightarrow\left(\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}+c_{0}\left[\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}-\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\right].

We also know, see (
7.2
), that there exist
\mathbf{f}
,
\mathbf{g}
,

\|\mathbf{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}=\|\mathbf{g}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}=1
, such that

(8.3)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\right){\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(I^{0})}\gtrsim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}.

In our example the functions
\mathbf{f}
and
\mathbf{g}
were explicitly constructed, but in what follows
we only need the estimate (
8.3
).

Trivially, by taking a slightly smaller implicit constant in the estimate (
8.3
) we can
always assume that
\mathbf{f}
and
\mathbf{g}
have finite Haar expansions (so (
8.2
)
also holds).

Let us show that the second term in (
8.2
) is negligible. For that it is enough to
show that for any
\mathbf{f}
,
\mathbf{g}
,
\|\mathbf{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}=\|\mathbf{g}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}=1
we have

(8.4)

\displaystyle\left|\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\right)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\right|,\ \left|\left(\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}},\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\right)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\right|\leq 4\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{1/2}.

But the quantity
\left\|\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\right\|
is exactly
the norm of the averaging operator
f\mapsto\mathbf{E}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}f=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\langle f\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}
, so for any

\hat{\mathbf{f}}
,
\hat{\mathbf{g}}

\displaystyle\left|\left(\langle\hat{\mathbf{f}}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}},\langle\hat{\mathbf{g}}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}\right)_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\right|\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{1/2}\|\hat{\mathbf{f}}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\|\hat{\mathbf{g}}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}.

Taking
\hat{\mathbf{f}}:=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\mathbf{f}
,
\hat{\mathbf{g}}:=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\mathbf{g}
we see that

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{f}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}=2\langle\hat{\mathbf{f}}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}},\qquad\langle\mathbf{g}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}=2\langle\hat{\mathbf{f}}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}}.

Noticing that
\|\hat{\mathbf{f}}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\leq\|\mathbf{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}
,
\|\hat{\mathbf{g}}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}\leq\|\mathbf{g}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}
we immediately get the first inequality in (
8.4
). Taking

\hat{\mathbf{f}}:=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\mathbf{f}
,
\hat{\mathbf{g}}:=\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\mathbf{g}
we get the second one.

Therefore the last term in (
8.2
) is bounded by
4\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{1/2}\ll\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}

so for large
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}
it is negligible. This means that for sufficiently large
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f},\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}\right)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}\gtrsim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}.

But by Lemma
7.6

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\varrho^{\vec{N}}W)}\longrightarrow\|\mathbf{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}=1,\qquad\left\|\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}\right\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}((\varrho^{\vec{N}}W)^{-1})}\longrightarrow\|\mathbf{g}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W^{-1})}=1,

so we got the desired lower bound (
8.1
) (with
\widetilde{\mathbf{f}}
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{g}}
being the
functions
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{f}
and

\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}^{\vec{N}}\mathbf{g}
normalized in
L^{2}(W)
and
L^{2}(W^{-1})
respectively).
∎

9.
Tracking the
\mathbf{A}_{2}
constant

In the above sections we constructed (for a sufficiently large
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}
) the weight
\widetilde{W}
on

I^{0}:=[0,1)
,
[\widetilde{W}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}
, and a function
\mathbf{f}\in L^{2}(\widetilde{W})
(supported on

I^{0}
),
\|f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(W)}\neq 0
such
that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{H}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\mathbf{f}\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\widetilde{W})}\gtrsim\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}^{3/2}\|f\|{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{L^{2}(\widetilde{W})}.

Extending this weight periodically to the real line, and slightly abusing notation using the same
symbol for it, it is trivial that the above inequality holds for the same function
\mathbf{f}

(supported on
I^{0}
, not extended periodically).

It is trivial that after extending periodically, the weight
\widetilde{W}
still satisfies the dyadic

\mathbf{A}_{2}

condition and that
[\widetilde{W}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}
. Here by the dyadic
\mathbf{A}_{2}
condition we mean
the condition (
3.1
) with
\mathscr{D}
being the collection of all dyadic subintervals of
\mathbb{R}
,
not just
\mathscr{D}(I^{0})
.

In the rest of this section we will follow this agreement and use
\mathscr{D}
for the collection of all
dyadic subintervals of
\mathbb{R}
.

We claim that our weight
\widetilde{W}
(periodically extended) satisfies, in fact, the standard
\mathbf{A}_{2}

condition, and that
[\widetilde{W}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}\leq 16^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}
.

9.1.
The strong dyadic
\mathbf{A}_{2}
condition

We will need the following definition.

Definition 9.1
.

We say that a weight
W
satisfies the
strong dyadic

\mathbf{A}_{2}
condition, denoted

W\in\mathbf{A}_{2}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}
, if the estimate (
1.1
) is satisfied for all intervals
I
that are a
union of 2 adjacent dyadic intervals of equal length. The corresponding supremum, the strong dyadic

\mathbf{A}_{2}

characteristic, will be denoted as
[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}
.

Note that any dyadic interval is a union of its children, so
trivially
[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm dy}}\leq[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\leq[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}
. The lemma below shows
that the classes
\mathbf{A}_{2}
and
\mathbf{A}_{2}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}
coincide, and that the corresponding characteristics
are equivalent.

Lemma 9.2
.

For a matrix weight
W
,

\displaystyle[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}\leq 16[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}.

Proof.

Take an arbitrary interval
J
(not necessarily dyadic), and let
I_{1}
be a dyadic interval such
that

\displaystyle|J|\leq|I_{1}|\leq 2|J|,\qquad I_{1}\cap J\neq\varnothing.

We can find a dyadic interval
I_{2}
adjacent to
I_{1}
, with

|I_{2}|=|I_{1}|
such that
J\subset I_{1}\cup I_{2}=:I
. Trivially
|I|\leq 4|J|
, so

(9.1)

\displaystyle\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}

\displaystyle\leq|J|^{-1}\int_{I}W(x){\mathrm{d}}x\leq 4\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\qquad

(9.2)

\displaystyle\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}

\displaystyle\leq 4\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}.

The inequality (
9.2
) is equivalent to

(9.3)

\displaystyle\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq 4\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}^{-1},

so using Lemma
3.2

we can write

\displaystyle\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}\leq 4\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\leq 4[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}\leq 16[W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\langle W^{-1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{-1}\,;

the second inequality here follows from Lemma
3.2
, the last one from
(
9.3
).

Applying Lemma
3.2
again we get the conclusion of the lemma.
∎

Below we will prove that
[\widetilde{W}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}
, which by Lemma
9.2
gives us
that
[\widetilde{W}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}\leq 16^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}
.

9.2.
Procedure
7.3
and strong dyadic
\mathbf{A}_{2}

Let us recall, see
Remark
7.5
, that the remodeled weight
\widetilde{W}
was obtained from the dyadic weight
W
by
starting with the dyadic weight
\mathbf{E}_{1}W:=\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{+}}+\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}\mathbf{1}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{0}_{-}}
, applying inductively Procedure
7.3
and taking the limit.

Note, that the inverse
\widetilde{W}^{-1}
of the remodeled weight is obtained exactly the same way from
the weight
W^{-1}
: start with the weight
\mathbf{E}_{1}(W^{-1})
and apply Procedure
7.3

exactly the same way.

That was discussed for the remodeling of a weight on the interval
I^{0}
, but the same can be said
about the periodic extension of the remodeled weight. Namely, this periodic extension can be
obtained from taking the weight
\mathbf{E}_{1}W
, extending it periodically, then applying steps
described in Procedure
7.3
to starting intervals and taking the limit.

To see that it can be done, take some numbering
I_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
of the dyadic intervals of length

1
, and start with the periodic extension of the weight
\mathbf{E}_{1}W
. Let us assume, without loss of
generality, that
I_{1}=I^{0}
, and let
I_{1,k}
be the (ordered) starting subintervals of
I_{1}
to
which we consecutively apply Procedure
7.3
to get the remodeled weight
\widetilde{W}
on
the interval
I_{1}
.

Let
I_{j,k}
be the translation of the starting interval
I_{1,k}
to the interval
I_{j}
.
Performing Procedure
7.3
consecutively on intervals
I_{1,1}
,
I_{1,2}
,
I_{2,1}
,

I_{1,3}
,
I_{2,2}
,
I_{3,1}
,
I_{1,4}
etc, gives us the periodic extension of the remodeled
weight.

Note that the inverse
\widetilde{W}^{-1}
of the (periodically extended) remodeled weight is obtained

exactly
the same way from the weight
V=W^{-1}
.

Since we will be dealing with the averages of
W
and
V=W^{-1}
, we will need an extra
definition.

Definition 9.3
.

We say that a pair of weights
V
,
W
satisfies the strong dyadic
\mathbf{A}_{2}
condition (and write

(V,W)\in\mathbf{A}_{2}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}
) if

\displaystyle\sup_{I}\|\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\|^{2}:=[V,W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}<\infty,

where the supremum is taken over all intervals
I
that are unions of two adjecent dyadic intervals
of equal length.

The quantity
[V,W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}
is called the
strong dyadic
\mathbf{A}_{2}
characteristic
of
the pair
V
,
W
.

The next lemma is crucial for controlling the
\mathbf{A}_{2}
characteristic.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
V
,
W
be matrix weights on
\mathbb{R}
such that
[V,W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}
. Assuming that

V
and
W
are constant on an interval
J\in\mathscr{D}
, define new weights
V_{1}
,
W_{1}
as

\displaystyle V_{1}:=V+\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\Psi,\qquad W_{1}:=W+\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\Phi,\qquad N\geq 2,

where
\Psi=\Psi^{*}
,
\Phi=\Phi^{*}
are some matrix-valued functions
5
5

5

Here we are implicitly assuming
V_{1}
,
W_{1}
are weights, i.e. that
\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}+\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\Psi\geq 0
,
\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}+\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\Phi\geq 0
on
J
.
.

If

(9.4)

\displaystyle\|\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}^{1/2}\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\!J}^{1/2}\|^{2}\leq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}},

then

\displaystyle[V_{1},W_{1}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}.

This lemma implies that
[\widetilde{W}]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}
, which is exactly what we need.

Proof of Lemma
9.4
.

Let
I_{1}
,
I_{2}
be two adjacent dyadic intervals of equal length, and let
I:=I_{1}\cup I_{2}
. We
want to show that

(9.5)

\displaystyle\|\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{1/2}\|^{2}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}.

First, notice that if
J\cap I=\varnothing
or
J\subset I
, then trivially

(9.6)

\displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},\qquad\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I},

so (
9.5
) holds (because
[V,W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}
).

It remains to consider the case
I\cap J\neq\varnothing
, but
J\not\subset I
. There are two
possibilities here:

(i)

J\not\subset I
and
I\not\subset J
;

(ii)

I\subsetneq J
.

In the first case one of the intervals (say
I_{1}
) does not intersect
J
, but touches its
boundary, and the other one (
I_{2}
) is strictly inside of
J
and touches the boundary of
J
at
the same point.

In this case the identity (
9.6
) holds for
I_{2}
, see Lemma
9.5
below.
For
I_{1}
it holds trivially (the weights do not change), so (
9.6
) holds for

I=I_{1}\cup I_{2}
.

Therefore, (
9.5
) holds for
I
because

[V,W]{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{\mathbf{A}_{2}}^{\scriptstyle\text{\rm sd}}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}
.

Finally, let us treat the case (ii),
I\subsetneq J
.
Writing
J
as a disjoint union

\displaystyle J=\bigcup_{k=1}^{2^{N+2}}J_{k}^{\prime},\qquad J_{k}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N+1}(J),

we can see that the weights
V_{1}
,
W_{1}
are constant on the intervals
J_{k}^{\prime}
.

Defining the auxiliary weights

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}:=\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}+\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\Psi,\qquad\widetilde{W}:=\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}+\Delta{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{2}\Phi,

we can see that for any
J_{k}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N+2}(J)
there exists
I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)\cup\{J\}
such that

\displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}}=\langle\widetilde{V}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}\,,\qquad\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}}=\langle\widetilde{W}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}};

(the case
I^{\prime}=J
happens when
J_{k}^{\prime}
is a subset of an exceptional stopping interval).

Since for any
\varphi\in L^{1}(J)

\displaystyle\langle\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)}\langle\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}},

we can see that for any
J_{k}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N+2}(J)
either

(9.7)

\displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\langle\widetilde{V}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\langle\widetilde{W}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}\quad\text{for some }I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)

or

(9.8)

\displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\langle\widetilde{V}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)}\langle\widetilde{V}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\langle\widetilde{W}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)}\langle\widetilde{W}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}\,.

Let again
n\in\mathbb{N}
be such that
I\in\operatorname{ch}^{n}(J)
. If
n\geq N+2
then since
V_{1}
,
W_{1}
are constant
on intervals
J_{k}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N+2}(J)
, we have

\displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}},\qquad\text{where\ }I\subset J_{k}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N+2}(J).

If
n<N+2
, then
I
is a disjoint union of some
J_{k}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N+2}(J)
, so
\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}
,

\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}
are the averages of
\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}}
,
\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}^{\prime}}
over these

J_{k}^{\prime}
. Combining this with (
9.7
), (
9.8
) we get that in any case

(9.9)

\displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)}\alpha{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}\langle\widetilde{V}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}},\qquad\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\sum_{I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)}\alpha{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}\langle\widetilde{W}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}},

where

\displaystyle\alpha{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\geq 0,\qquad\sum_{I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)}\alpha{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}=1.

But for any
I^{\prime}\in\operatorname{ch}^{2}(J)

\displaystyle\langle\widetilde{V}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}\leq 4\langle\widetilde{V}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}=4\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}\,,\qquad\langle\widetilde{W}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I^{\prime}}\leq 4\langle\widetilde{W}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}=4\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}

so

\displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\leq 4\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}\,,\qquad\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}

\displaystyle\leq 4\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}.

The second inequality is equivalent to

\displaystyle\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq 4\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1},

so we can write

\displaystyle\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\leq 4\langle V\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}\leq 4\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\langle W\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{-1}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1};

here in the second inequality we used Lemma
3.1
.

Applying Lemma
3.1
to the resulting inequality
\langle V_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}\leq 16\boldsymbol{\mathcal{Q}}\langle W_{1}\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}^{-1}
we get the desired estimate (
9.5
).
∎

The following lemma applies to arbitrary
L^{1}
functions, not only to weights.

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
I
be a dyadic subinterval of an interval
J
, touching its boundary, and let
\varphi\in L^{1}(J)
.
Then for any
N\geq 2

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\langle\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}.

Proof.

The case
I=J
is trivial, so we assume that
I\subsetneq J
, so
I
touches the boundary of
J
at
one point. Let
J_{0}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N}(J)
touch the boundary of
J
at the same point as
I
.

Let
n
be such that
I\in\operatorname{ch}^{n}(J)
. If
n\geq N
, then
I\subset J_{0}
, and since
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\varphi

is constant on
J_{0}
we conclude that

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{I}=\langle\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{0}}=\langle\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J};

the last equality holds by the construction of
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\varphi
.

If
n<N
then
I
can be represented as a disjoint union

\displaystyle I=\bigcup_{k=0}^{m}J_{k},\qquad J_{k}\in\operatorname{ch}^{N}(J).

By the construction of
\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\varphi

\langle\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{P}{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J}^{N}\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J_{k}}=\langle\varphi\rangle{\vphantom{\rule[-2.79857pt]{0.0pt}{1.50696pt}}}_{J},

and we immediately get the conclusion of the lemma.
∎

We used this Lemma in the proof of Lemma
9.4
, which is now completely proved. Tracking the strong dyadic
\mathbf{A}_{2}
characteristic of the remodeled weight
\tilde{W}
is accomplished. This finishes the construction of our counterexample to the matrix
\mathbf{A}_{2}
conjecture.
